@@138963316 <h> Microplastics could accelerate antibiotic resistance , studies find <h> Plastic pollution and drug-resistant infections are usually regarded as separate global crises . But emerging research suggests links between them : Microplastic particles in the environment are colonised by bacteria , and those bacteria develop antibiotic resistance at an unprecedented rate . <p> Research into the link between microplastics and drug resistance is ongoing , but action is needed now , say experts . Limiting opportunities for bacteria , antibiotics and microplastics to mix with each other , via better wastewater management and surveillance of drug-resistant strains , may be the best hope . Image : Surfrider Foundation Oregon Chapters , CC BY-SA 3.0 , via Flickr . <p> Simultaneously , the ability of disease-causing bacteria to withstand one or more antibiotics ( known as antimicrobial resistance , or AMR ) has surged to become a public health emergency now accounting for around 5 million deaths worldwide annually . <p> " AMR is an existential human threat , " says Tim Walsh , a professor at the University of Oxford and director of biology at the UK 's Ineos Oxford @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ video call . " It will kill more people each year than TB , HIV and malaria , and if unchallenged could eclipse cancer as the biggest killer . " <p> Until very recently , these two global crises , plastic pollution and antimicrobial resistance , were considered separately by scientists and policymakers . But a new line of research suggests they 're inextricably linked : <p> Plastic waste is quickly colonised by microorganisms , creating a new type of ecosystem dubbed the " plastisphere . " And bacteria living in the plastisphere are developing greater resistance to antibiotics at an unprecedented rate . <h> " <p> We know that microplastics are ingested at the base of the food chain by filter feeders , like mussels , if there 's any harmful pathogens or microplastics in there , they could be making their way into the human food chain . <p> Emily Stevenson , researcher , University of Exeter <h> How microplastics enhance antimicrobial resistance <p> In 2025 , researchers at Boston University found that Escherichia coli bacteria exposed to microplastics developed enhanced resistance to four common antibiotics when compared @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 10 days of microplastic exposure , the bacteria 's tolerance to antibiotics " just shot up , " reaching 100 times the level it had started at , says Neila Gross , the doctoral researcher who led the study , in a video call with Mongabay . " This was very , very concerning . " <p> Researchers are still unravelling exactly how and why microplastics enhance antimicrobial resistance . A key piece of the puzzle is the way bacteria behave when colonising a surface . Although we usually think of bacteria as solitary , when they grow on a surface they stick together and form a complex community known as a biofilm . <p> Biofilm bacteria secrete a protective gel-like material made of proteins , carbohydrates and DNA , which helps them stay attached to the surface they 're on and provides protection against harmful substances , including antibiotics . " It 's a physical barrier , sort of like armour , and the bacteria hang out in the middle of it , " Gross says . <p> Biofilm bacteria are more likely to develop resistance to antibiotics than @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ most , but not all , of the antibiotics that are trying to break through . This means the bacteria are exposed to antibiotics at levels high enough to prompt a defensive response , but not high enough to kill them outright . Much like an incomplete course of antibiotics , this low-level exposure primes the bacteria to evolve resistance . <p> By promoting the formation of stronger , more stable biofilms , microplastics create the perfect environment for drug resistance to develop . <p> Separate studies conducted in China and the UK have found that , in river environments , microplastic waste can harbor more antibiotic-resistant bacteria and a higher abundance of some resistance genes , compared to naturally occurring surfaces like wood or rock . <h> Microplastics encourage gene sharing <p> Another reason drug resistance can spread so rapidly through biofilms is that the bacteria residing in such films readily exchange genetic material via a process known as horizontal gene transfer . Instead of every bacterium having to individually evolve resistance to every antibiotic from scratch through chance mutations , a resistance gene evolved by one bacterium can @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are like these genetic sponges , and their DNA is very plastic , " Walsh says . " They 're able to grow together , communicate together , and exchange genetic information far more readily than human beings . They are a formidable foe in that regard . " <h> Some plastic types are higher risk <p> Scientists are now working to understand what specific plastic characteristics make them effective at promoting drug resistance . What we know so far is that certain types of plastics seem to be more problematic than others . <p> Research also suggests that expanded polystyrene , the type used to make packing peanuts , may be at higher risk of fostering drug-resistant bacteria than other plastic types . " The very nature of plastics makes them a risky substrate , but polystyrene is a very complex matrix , " study lead author Emily Stevenson , a PhD researcher at the UK 's University of Exeter , tells Mongabay in a video call . " That porous structure gives more surface area for colonisation of bacteria . " <p> A further complication : Plastic particles @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ water . This ageing process can make their exteriors rougher , creating a bigger surface area on which bacterial biofilms can stick . Ageing also causes toxic chemicals to leach out of plastics , which can trigger the bacteria 's S.O.S response , helping resistance genes spread . <h> Microplastics could carry resistant bacteria to new locations <p> Once AMR has developed in an environment , humans can contract drug-resistant infections through contact with contaminated water , soil or food . Microplastics may play a role here too : Lightweight plastics , such as expanded polystyrene , can be carried in storm runoff , in streams or by ocean currents , potentially relocating antibiotic-resistant bacteria into new environments . <p> " We know that microplastics are ingested at the base of the food chain by filter feeders , like mussels , " Stevenson says . Her PhD research suggests that microplastics could increase the amount of AMR genes in the mussels ' guts . Since humans eat shellfish like mussels whole , " if there 's any harmful pathogens or microplastics in there , they could be making their way @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ More research is needed to confirm and expand on these preliminary findings . <h> Wastewater : A hotspot for drug-resistance <p> Bacteria are ubiquitous in the global environment , as are microplastics . But there are locations where they come together in high concentrations , alongside antibiotics and other toxic substances , creating AMR hotspots . <p> Advanced wastewater treatment plants ( WWTPs ) filter and treat raw sewage to remove bacteria , pharmaceutical residues and pollutants , including microplastics . Though this process can be very effective , it 's not perfect . <p> " Plastics break down into smaller and smaller pieces , which are really difficult to filter or remove from the wastewater , " Tam Tran , a senior researcher at the Norwegian research institute ( NORCE ) , says in a video call with Mongabay . <p> Wastewater , even after going through an advanced treatment facility , presents a risk for AMR development . But when sanitation facilities are limited , inadequate or missing entirely , for example , in disadvantaged communities , war zones or refugee camps , there is an even greater @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ you have degradation of infrastructure ... there is that direct link to the carriage of resistant bacteria , " Walsh says . <p> In high-income countries , drug-resistant infections are a big problem , but one that can often be overcome with medical care . In low- and middle-income countries , where sanitation facilities and water treatment plants are less available , multidrug-resistant infections are prevalent , and often fatal . <p> In many African and Southeast Asian nations , " we 've already run out of antibiotics to choose from , and of course , it affects the poorest population , " Walsh says . " It 's directly linked to poverty . " <p> Low- and middle-income countries also often have fewer recycling facilities , which means plastic waste is also more likely to end up in open-air dumps and streams , on beaches , and in the street , greately expanding the plastisphere colonised by bacteria . <h> Multiple crises combine to promote drug resistance <p> Plastics and antibiotics are both manufactured substances introduced by humans into the environment . They can both affect the functioning of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ For this reason , they are grouped together in the " novel entities " planetary boundary , one of nine boundaries that , if breached , threaten to push Earth out of the habitable zone . Scientists say that humanity has now exceeded safe thresholds for six of the nine boundaries , including the novel entities boundary . <p> There may also be indirect climate effects : Extreme heat , humidity , flooding and drought brought by global warming can negatively impact water quality and food security , increasing the prevalence of infectious diseases and necessitating more use of antibiotics , which promotes AMR . <p> " How we treat our environment , both in terms of environmental degradation , but also in terms of climate change , is directly linked to AMR , " Walsh says . <p> Most plastics are produced from fossil fuels , which need to be extracted and pumped via pipes to refineries . Biocides , chemical substances used to kill microorganisms such as bacteria , are used in those pipes to prevent the buildup of bacterial biofilm , which could clog the pipes . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of bacteria , their systemic industrial use could , in fact , encourage AMR development . Chemical additives used in the plastic manufacturing process may also promote drug resistance . Again , more research is needed to fully understand these risks . <p> Collecting and transporting plastic waste , whether moving it to landfills or recycling plants , could transport drug-resistant bacteria to new environments . Even recycling may promote AMR , because plastic waste is often treated with disinfectants before being processed , with any surviving bacteria potentially becoming resistant . <p> " Plastics and AMR are fundamentally linked , " Stevenson says . " At every stage of the life cycle of plastics , from production to disposal , there is something that could influence antimicrobial resistance . " <p> However , plastic can also be a useful tool in the battle against AMR . " One of the really beneficial uses of plastics is that they keep things sterile , " Stevenson says , which reduces the transmission of drug-resistant bacteria in hospitals and clinics . " It 's sort of a double-edged sword . " <h> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ deeply concerning link between microplastics and AMR , potentially exacerbated by other environmental crises such as climate change . But many question remain : <p> How do biofilms on microplastics change and evolve over time ? How do chemical additives -- tens of thousands of which are routinely added to different types of plastic -- influence AMR ? What happens when other substances in the environment , ranging from heavy metals to plant secretions , interact with biofilm-laden microplastics ? And how do microplastics in our digestive system influence drug resistance in the microbiome ? <p> While scientists work to answer these and many other questions regarding microplastics and AMR , public health experts recognise that action is desperately needed to stop the spread of drug-resistant bacteria and to control the flood of plastic waste into the environment . <p> A good place to start would be " reducing the amount of plastics and antibiotics going into the wastewater treatment plant ... so using less plastics and having better antimicrobial stewardship , " Stevenson says . " We really need to make sure we 're not just regulating one of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . " <p> Slowing AMR spread is just another in a long list of reasons humanity needs to break free from the cycle of plastic manufacture and waste . But , with so much plastic already in the environment , it may be too little , too late . <p> Limiting opportunities for the mixing of this dangerous bacterial , antibiotic and microplastic cocktail by utilising better wastewater management and ongoing AMR surveillance may be the best hope for global public health and safety . <h> Like this content ? @@138993963 <h> The world 's superpowers are scrambling for an edge . It makes all of us less safe <p> Addressing economic issues could be the starting point to repairing the more recent geopolitical cracks , says the writer . <p> PHOTO : REUTERS <p> Pierre-Olivier Gourinchas <p> Published Jul 14 , 2026 , 05:00 PM <p> Updated Jul 15 , 2026 , 09:24 AM <p> Barely a month after I became the economic counsellor to the International Monetary Fund in 2022 , Russian tanks rolled into Ukraine . The Russian invasion would pose a challenge I had n't quite anticipated : managing the economic fallout of wars . Since then , the global economy has had to navigate a series of conflicts -- economic ones , like US President Donald Trump 's volley of tariffs , and actual ones , like the war in the Middle East involving the United States , Israel and Iran . <p> Too often , these shocks are viewed as isolated disruptions . They are not . They are interconnected symptoms of a deeper fragmentation reshaping the global economy . This fragmentation , both geopolitical @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ new age of war . One defined not necessarily by constant military confrontation but by a persistent undercurrent of strategic economic rivalry and coercion and rising economic insecurity . And , yes , also increased risks of actual wars . <p> Driving this transformation is a shift in the world 's centre of gravity . For decades , the global economic order was relatively stable , with the US at its centre , underpinned by a broad commitment among nations to economic integration . <p> The IMF , with its 191 member countries , is a demonstration of this cohesiveness . That system fostered a remarkable expansion in trade , investment and living standards . International trade , now nearing US$35 trillion ( S$45.3 trillion ) annually , increased roughly twentyfold since 1960 , and the share of the world 's population living in extreme poverty fell from about 43 per cent in 1990 to just 10 per cent today . <p> We should n't be surprised that the world 's centre of gravity is shifting . That 's consistent with poorer economies growing and catching up to their developed @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a multipolar one -- China obviously comes to mind , but many other advanced and emerging economies across Asia , Latin America and Eastern Europe have benefited from this more than 60-year expansion . <p> With that shift , geopolitical pressures are intensifying , and the system is now under strain . That 's the paradox . Economic integration has delivered substantial gains and created mutual interests that act as a force for peace . <p> Yet integration creates interdependence and , with it , vulnerabilities . It exposes countries to supply disruptions and to trade or financial choke points in energy , critical minerals or advanced computer chips . And where there are choke points , there is leverage to exploit , as Iran recently demonstrated . <p> The result is a dangerous feedback loop . As countries seek to insulate themselves from perceived risks , they risk fragmenting the global economy further still . This , in turn , encourages further insulation efforts -- through tariffs , industrial policy , financial regulation , export controls or rising military spending . <p> We have seen such dynamics before . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the 20th century , at the height of a British-led expansion of trade , capital flows and immigration . What followed was a period of intense deglobalisation , coinciding with rising nationalism and militarisation -- and two world wars . To assume that today 's economic integration -- and peace -- are here to stay would be complacent . <p> The global economy has proved remarkably resilient so far . In response to the Iran war , energy markets have adjusted and financial markets have remained calm . Countries such as China , Japan , South Korea and the US cushioned the disruption by tapping oil reserves or switching energy sources . Despite escalating trade tensions , global trade did not decline but strengthened in 2025 . Instead , countries and firms adjusted trade routes and supply chains . Shocks that might once have triggered systemic breakdowns have instead been absorbed . <p> But do n't mistake resilience for invulnerability . Risks are mounting beneath the surface . Financial systems built on deep integration -- notably around the US dollar -- could prove harder to sustain in a fragmented @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on one another or , in Canada 's case , the US , new fault lines may emerge , particularly as rapid technological transformations introduce fresh vulnerabilities around cryptocurrencies , critical minerals and artificial intelligence . <p> The deeper drivers of fragmentation are domestic . Across many countries , most visibly in richer ones , too many citizens feel that globalisation has not worked for them . Inequalities in income , opportunity and economic security have fuelled grievances . Those who bear the brunt of the losses rarely forget them . These political tensions are as important as any geopolitical rivalry and can fuel them in turn . <p> The question is whether the multipolar world we inhabit tomorrow will take the form of opposing blocs , whose contours have yet to emerge -- witness the ructions among members at the latest NATO summit -- or a more cooperative system built on shared rules and continued integration . Without a course correction , we risk drifting towards division . <p> Increasingly , the world 's superpowers are searching for strategic advantages , identifying choke points , adopting inward-looking policies and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and sovereignty . These actions may be individually rational , but collectively they make the world less secure , less prosperous and less stable . <p> Economics is not everything . Addressing economic issues , though , could be the starting point to repairing the more recent geopolitical cracks . It begins with acknowledging the risks . In a world of genuine strategic competition and coercion , we would be naive to pretend nothing needs to change . Yet fragmentation does n't have to be our destiny . The challenge is not whether to build resilience , but how to do so . <p> A key economic lesson in international trade is that size matters . Countries can work to rebuild economic gravity by joining clusters of cooperation with shared rules and mutual trust that enhance resilience while preserving openness . At the same time , domestic policies must deliver stable and balanced growth at home . Strengthening social protection and ensuring fair opportunities are essential for both domestic and global cohesion . <p> Importantly , the evidence suggests that economic instruments of coercion such as sanctions , tariffs and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ often accelerate fragmentation and invite retaliation . Ultimately , this is self-defeating . These tools should be used sparingly . <p> Lastly , international institutions ca n't retreat in the face of these challenges ; instead , cooperation must adapt and deepen . Shared issues such as AI , financial stability , climate change and migration can not be managed by countries acting alone . A multipolar world requires more coordination , not less . <p> The IMF still has a critical role to play . It helps countries build stronger economies , and with more than US$123 billion in current lending commitments , it provides financial support in times of stress and offers a platform where cooperation remains possible even when international relations become strained . <p> It was from the ashes of World War II , one of the most tragic periods of our modern history , that institutions such as the IMF were born , with a mandate to promote global economic cooperation , thus contributing to growth and shared prosperity . That ideal remains as relevant today as ever . A new age of war is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Gourinchas is a professor of economics at the University of California , Berkeley . This article originally appeared in The New York Times .

" <p> The consultancy added that it would prioritise helping employees transition into new roles . In Singapore , the delay to mandatory climate disclosure rules has also reduced immediate pressure on companies to invest in sustainability consulting support . <p> Founded to help organisations and individuals " navigate change with intention " , TTP built a reputation in Singapore 's sustainability scene through climate awareness events , workshops and strategic advisory work . " Awareness events and sustainability action adoption are decreasing everywhere I look , and people feel an overwhelming sense of anxiety . " <p> He characterised the mood among some practitioners as entering " a mourning phase " . <p> " We know it 's too late now , that no one cares much , no one wants to change and that it 's time to let go , " he said . " It 's a new feeling I hear about in conversations -- and it 's extremely worrying . " <p> Meurgue-Guyard likened sustainability professionals to Cassandra of Troy , a tragic figure of the unheard truth from Greek mythology . " We 're all trying to be heard , but to no avail . @@139025227 <p> Forest canopies create the conditions for an extraordinary diversity of life to thrive . By casting shade and retaining moisture in the air and soil , they generate cool and stable understory environments . In the tropics , this regulating effect is particularly crucial for countless species that otherwise would be unable to survive in the extreme heat of surrounding open landscapes . <p> However , forest scientists are increasingly concerned that the combined pressures of global warming and habitat degradation could undermine forests ' critical thermal buffering capacity . <p> New research from Southeast Asia predicts forests across the region will experience unprecedented peak temperatures over the next three decades . Heat levels beneath the canopy could hit new highs even within currently intact ecosystems by 2050 , the study found , potentially exposing forest-dwelling animals , plants and fungi to severe levels of heat stress . <p> " Our findings show that climate change is not only warming the atmosphere globally , but also reshaping the local conditions that species experience inside forests , " said Erone Ghizoni Santos , who led the study while he was @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <h> " <p> Tropical forest microclimate data are still quite sparse , particularly across Southeast Asia , where many forests are remote and difficult to access . By establishing a systematic network of field measurements across a large geographic area , the study helps close a major data gap . <p> Vivienne Groner , scientist , Imperial College London <h> Region-wide warming <p> Prior research in Malaysian Borneo has demonstrated that when forests are degraded , such as through logging , they become more affected by climate shifts in the surrounding landscape . Depleted canopies allow more sunlight to reach the ground and trap less moisture , allowing heat to permeate the ecosystem . <p> What was missing , according to Santos , was an understanding of how the thermal buffering capacity of forest canopies is likely to shift under future development and climate change scenarios . <p> In the new study , published in Geophysical Research Letters ' , Santos and his colleagues combined understory temperature measurements from 46 forest sites across Southeast Asia with satellite data and climate model projections to make predictions about future forest microclimate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , by 2050 , peak daytime temperatures inside forests could increase by an average of between 1.4 ? Celsius ( 2.52 ? Fahrenheit ) and 2.1 ? C ( 3.78 ? F ) across the region , depending on the climate model used . " In several parts of the region , forests may experience temperatures higher than anything recorded in recent decades , " Santos said . <p> Under a " middle of the road " climate mitigation pathway where moderate efforts are made to curb emissions , the study estimated more than half the region could see peak temperatures beneath the forest canopy exceed historical maximums . Under a high-emissions climate scenario , the affected area would increase to two-thirds of the region . <p> The scale of the projected warming surprised even the researchers , Santos said , and feeds into concerns from conservationists about how species will cope with future environmental conditions . Even modest increases in forest understory temperatures could be enough to push some species beyond their physiological tolerance . <p> Species that are unable to move to cooler habitats or have limited ability @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be impacted the most , Santos said . <p> In terms of regional patterns , the study models indicated that forests at higher altitudes across the region , including in mountainous parts of Myanmar and Laos , might escape the brunt of future thermal stress . This could be due to the overall cooling influence of elevation , Santos and his colleagues suggested in the study . <p> Conversely , the models predicted lowland areas of northern Cambodia , southeastern Indonesia , and the island of Borneo will experience the strongest increases in temperature . Without sincere action to curb development pressure and greenhouse gas emissions , forests in these areas could see peak daytime temperatures increase by as much as 4 ? C ( 7.2 ? F ) above baseline levels , according to the study . <h> Cascading effects of microclimate shifts <p> While the findings ultimately underscored the need to curb global fossil fuel emissions to mitigate future warming , the research team said their findings can help guide conservation action . <p> They recommended policymakers and conservation practitioners adopt a spatial prioritisation approach to shore up @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Intact forests , which provide stronger thermal buffering and may also serve as climate refuges for biodiversity , should also be protected , he said . In addition , vulnerable regions expected to undergo significant future warming could benefit from conservation attention to restore forest structure and enhance landscape connectivity . <p> Vivienne Groner , an Earth system scientist at Imperial College London who was n't involved in the study , said the " scale and consistency " of the new study 's approach deepens the understanding of how forest microclimates are likely to shift under future climate change . <p> " Tropical forest microclimate data are still quite sparse , particularly across Southeast Asia , where many forests are remote and difficult to access , " Groner said . " By establishing a systematic network of field measurements across a large geographic area , the study helps close a major data gap . " <p> Ultimately , however , the impacts of changing forest conditions are likely to extend beyond individual species . The effects will " cascade throughout ecosystems , " she said , affecting food availability , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ capacity of forests to store carbon . " Over time , this could reduce ecosystem resilience and the services these forests provide . " <p> Groner said that while the study offers important insights into how forest temperatures could shift across the region , a fuller picture of how forest species will be affected will require studies that examine additional microclimate factors , such as humidity , wind , solar exposure , and physical feedbacks within ecosystems . <p> " There is real potential to link this rich thermal microclimate dataset with ecological and physiological research , " she said , " helping to bridge the gap between environmental change and ecosystem responses . " <h> Like this content ? " Tu asks with a grin . " That 's what I aim for -- a coffee that defies all perceptions of Vietnam 's robusta . Producers are also grappling with stricter sustainability requirements under the European Union 's Deforestation Regulation , rising input costs , and growing demands for traceability , she says . <p> " In the long term , Vietnam 's coffee producers should not just try to increase export volumes , but also shift from selling raw materials to a value-added model , " she adds . <h> Pride of the Highlands <p> That shift is already under way . <p> " Vietnam is the world 's largest producer of robusta coffee . " <p> In Thao 's opinion , with climate change disrupting global coffee supplies , robusta should no longer be just a blending bean . <p> " It is a climate-resilient crop with immense potential in the premium and speciality segments , " she adds . <h> More on this topic <p> Another company betting on premium robusta is Bazan Heritage Coffee , which is investing 300 billion dong in a large-scale facility in Dak Lak that includes an Italian-made coffee-processing line worth ? 3.5 million ( S$5.2 million ) . <p> " We want to build a closed value chain , from raw material sourcing and deep processing to brand development and the expansion of export markets , " says company chairman Dao Duc Dien . " All to the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " <p> Born and bred in Buon Ma Thuot , Dien , 44 , has been involved in coffee farming from a young age . <p> " Our robusta coffee is very different from that of other countries , thanks to the altitude of our farms , which are located 500m to 800m above sea level , " he explains . " The fertile , red basalt soil is another God-given advantage that gives the coffee its rich aroma and high caffeine content . " <p> Yet King Coffee 's CEO points out that transitioning from raw exports to premium products and services is a long process . <p> " This requires a unified strategy among farmers , enterprises , researchers , and policymakers to elevate Vietnamese coffee in the global value chain , " she stresses . <p> Currently , King Coffee products are sold in more than 60 countries including Singapore , and Thao is promoting a Global Coffee Alliance to deepen collaboration across the industry . Fast forward more than 30 years , and Highlands Coffee has opened its 1,000th store in June 2026 . It has more than 12,000 employees and serves over 100 million cups of coffee a year . <p> While Highlands Coffee hopes eventually to expand further overseas -- including to Singapore -- Thai says Vietnam remains its home and " most important responsibility " . <p> " From the beginning , that is what we have tried to protect at Highlands Coffee -- not only the flavour of Vietnamese coffee , but also its soul . @@139055662 <p> Thailand has asked Japan to support four new water management projects aimed at improving flood prevention , including measures for the flood-prone Chao Phraya River basin , as the Southeast Asian nation seeks to strengthen resilience against increasingly frequent extreme weather . <p> Deputy prime minister Songsak Thongsri said after talks on Wednesday with Japanese ambassador Masato Otaka that Bangkok had proposed four technical cooperation projects to the Japan International Cooperation Agency ( JICA ) , covering flood forecasting , river management and public participation in water governance . <p> The proposals include a decision-support system for the U-Tapao sub-basin , a water-zoning and floodplain management system for the Chao Phraya basin , the use of advanced technology to assess sediment accumulation at the mouth of the Chao Phraya River , and a public participation platform for water management . <p> Japan had shown particular interest in flood prevention initiatives for the Chao Phraya River basin and the southern city of Hat Yai , Songsak said . <p> He said Japan and JICA had long been key partners in helping Thailand improve water management , providing technical assistance following @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ flooding in Hat Yai last year . <p> " Both sides agreed on the need for a systematic approach to addressing water-related challenges , " Songsak said . <p> The renewed cooperation comes as Thailand continues to grapple with rising flood risks linked to rapid urbanisation , changing land use and increasingly erratic rainfall driven by climate change . <p> The 2011 floods , among the country 's worst natural disasters , killed more than 800 people , affected over 13 million across 65 provinces and caused economic losses estimated at about THB1.43 trillion ( US$45.7 billion ) , disrupting global supply chains as industrial estates north of Bangkok were inundated . <p> The disaster prompted Thailand and JICA to develop a comprehensive flood management master plan for the Chao Phraya River basin , which remains the backbone of the country 's long-term flood strategy . The plan combines structural measures , including water retention areas and bypass channels , with improved forecasting , land-use planning and emergency management . <p> The World Bank said in its latest Thailand Climate and Development Report that the Chao Phraya River basin remains @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a multi-billion-dollar programme designed to protect against major flood events . The bank said faster implementation of integrated flood management , improved hydrometeorological monitoring and better coordination between agencies would be critical to reducing future losses . <p> Bangkok , much of which lies on the low-lying Chao Phraya delta , also remains highly exposed to flooding from heavy rainfall , upstream river flows and high tides . The city 's drainage system was originally designed mainly for localised flooding and proved insufficient during the 2011 disaster , prompting authorities to expand drainage tunnels , pumping stations and water retention capacity . <p> Climate experts have warned that warming temperatures are increasing the intensity of heavy rainfall events across Southeast Asia , raising the urgency of strengthening flood forecasting , river basin management and community preparedness in one of the region 's most flood-exposed countries . <h> Like this content ? The recent energy crisis sent prices soaring and constrained many aspects of life and work in a region expected to account for 20 per cent of global energy demand growth in the coming decade . <p> As a global financial centre and leader in green capital , and ahead of its upcoming Asean chairmanship in 2027 , Singapore provides an opportune setting to convene experts , practitioners , advocates , and emerging leaders @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The training offers an opportunity to collaborate on shared climate challenges while accelerating the practical solutions needed to address them . <p> " Singapore 's role as a leading hub of clean energy innovation and financer in the region makes it an ideal place to convene the next generation of climate leaders and build the momentum for action that the climate crisis demands , " said former US Vice President Al Gore . @@139083521 <h> Why water and sanitation are women 's rights issues <p> You drink , bathe , cook , clean , flush , create , and grow with it . You may get it from a tap , bottle , bucket , river , or well . <p> Water -- unpolluted , adequate , and affordable -- is essential to the full enjoyment of our lives . <p> It is a human right and it is fundamental , in obvious and not-so-obvious ways . Access to clean water , as well as sanitation , may influence whether a girl can go to school , if a pregnant woman can deliver a healthy baby , or if a family has enough food to eat . <p> Water and sanitation -- or the lack of them -- impact the safety , agency , and time of women and girls all over the world . <p> Thanks to the tireless efforts of feminist advocates , we understand that water and sanitation are women 's rights issues . They carry the burden of collecting clean water for their families , endure harassment on their journeys @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ economic and social limitations because of period poverty . These problems persist in both urban and rural settings . <p> The international community and local communities further mark its importance with World Water Day ( and World Water Week ) , World Toilet Day , and Menstrual Hygiene Day through conferences , ceremonies , and events . <p> But how we officially measure progress on SDG6 does not include gender-based benchmarks -- part of a troubling absence of gender perspectives across WASH policies . Without explicitly addressing the rights and realities that women and girls face daily , access to water will remain deeply unequal . <p> This is where the work of UN Women and our partners is critical : We ensure women and girls -- their needs , rights , and leadership -- are not an invisible part of the global water crisis or its solutions . <h> What the water crisis looks like for women and girls <p> Intensifying climate change , environmental degradation , and growing demands from agriculture and industry have produced a global water crisis . This water crisis worsens under mismanagement and short-term @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ harming their health , safety , and opportunities . <p> By improving access to water and sanitation -- understanding how it goes hand-in-hand with gender equality -- we can drastically improve the lived experiences of women and girls around the world . <h> Clean water can mean the difference between life and death during childbirth <p> Every year , around one million mothers and babies die because of unclean births . Mothers in low- and middle-income countries or in conflict zones , where healthcare facilities have fewer resources , are most at risk of infection . <p> With clean and adequate water and sanitation , many of these deaths could be prevented . <h> The responsibility of collecting water still falls mostly on women , particularly those living in rural areas <p> In seven out of 10 households with no running water , women and girls are responsible for collecting it . Based on 53 countries where data is available , the time spent by women and girls is three times as much as men and boys . <p> Those hours add up to less time for education , paid @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ journey back and forth to water resources also exposes women and girls to harassment , assault , and exploitation . <p> Investments in infrastructure are investments in the lives of women and girls . Achieving SDG6 by 2030 will require US$1 trillion in financing per year . <h> The safety risks women and girls face without proper sanitation <p> Adequate and dignified sanitation is about having access to facilities where humans can do the most natural things we do , safely and privately . That means working toilets or latrines at home , school , and work ; doors that lock ; and a way to clean our hands to prevent disease . <p> For women and girls , who typically need to urinate more often than men and boys , the differences are n't just anatomical . When they are forced to use inadequate , unsecured , or distant facilities , they risk being exposed to harassment or violence . When there are no public sanitation facilities , women and girls participate less in public life . <p> And for menstruating or pregnant people , safe facilities are even @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ periods are wrongly stigmatised and menstruating women and girls are shunned by their families or societies . Lack of proper sanitation , lack of understanding , and unaffordable ( or unavailable ) period products mean that millions live in period poverty , especially in rural areas . <h> How water scarcity fuels hunger and food insecurity <p> When water and food become scarce , gender inequalities often deepen . Scarcity means that the women -- often responsible for gathering and managing water , food , and fuel for their families -- must work harder and travel farther . <p> Worse still , failure to provide can lead to physical violence , perpetuated by intimate partners or relations . <p> Gender inequality exists from the very beginning of the food supply chain . Women farmers have less access to land , financing , and information . Care responsibilities may mean they have less time to farm . So even in countries where women represent the majority of agricultural workers , they produce 20 to 30 per cent less than male counterparts . Water scarcity depletes from those already-thin margins . <h> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ displacement <p> Extreme events like flooding and drought -- which are intensified by fossil-fuel-driven climate change and cycles like El Ni ? o and La Ni ? a -- are harrowing experiences on their own . These crises deplete water supplies , disrupt services , damage infrastructure , and increase illness , hunger , displacement , and conflict . <p> Water-related disasters , which are growing more frequent and severe , account for 70 per cent of disaster-related deaths . <p> And for women and girls , evidence shows that these events increase the risk of violence . Rates of child marriage and forced marriage also rise , as families desperately cope with water scarcity , food insecurity , and poverty . <p> In a study on drought in Zimbabwe , Mozambique , and Lesotho , researchers found drastic increases in sexual violence ( 46 per cent ) , with adolescent girls most at risk . According to the IPCC , already-scarce water supplies in Southern Africa are expected to decline 30 per cent by 2050 . <h> The gender data gap leaves water solutions half empty <p> Data does @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ It lights the path to solutions . <p> Disaggregated data and gender statistics can show us , in hard numbers , the different impacts on women and men , girls and boys . Properly investing in it is the first step in ensuring no one is left behind in the race to meet SDG6 . <p> The most effective WASH policies use gender analysis and sex- , age- , and disability-disaggregated data ( SADDD ) to reflect the diverse experiences of women and girls . <h> Women are leading water solutions , but often excluded from decision-making <p> Despite the responsibilities that women and girls carry -- sometimes literally -- in managing water , their contributions are rarely recognised in the formal spaces where WASH policies are made . Women are largely excluded from community water governance , planning , and leadership roles . <p> Women and women-led organisations , including human rights advocates and Indigenous water defenders , bring critical experience and perspectives to the management of water . Feminist water governance strengthens water access , sustainability , and equality . <h> What real gender-responsive water and sanitation policies @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ : <p> Uphold women 's human rights , especially in conflict and disaster where resources are most scarce . <p> Promote women 's leadership and their full , equal , and meaningful participation -- including in rural and underserved communities -- through greater representation in decision-making , training , and financing . <p> Are built on disaggregated data and gender statistics , so policies reflect the diverse needs of the people they serve . <p> Recognise and redistribute women and girls ' unpaid care , domestic responsibilities , and community work , including the collection of water . <p> Invest in water and sanitation infrastructure closer to or within homes to reduce women and girls ' time constraints and drudgery , while ensuring greater safety and privacy .

@@139160310 <h> How a Philippine community is protecting sea turtle hatchlings <h> The municipality of San Vicente in the Philippines ' Palawan is a vital nesting ground for Olive Ridley sea turtles . <p> Nesting turtles and their eggs face an array of challenges . Predation by dogs is the most immediate threat , but rising sea levels , more severe storms and tourism development plans also loom large . Image : Dale de Vera , CC BY-SA 3.0 , via Unsplash . <p> When Jerald Mahusay was a kid , he and his friends saw turtle eggs so often , they treated them like toys . <p> " We were always playing with the eggs , " Mahusay recalled of his childhood in Santo Ni ? o , a village in the Philippines ' Palawan province . " We did n't know why they are important . " <p> Mahusay 's relationship with turtles has changed . In 2023 , he started working as a patroller for the Large Marine Vertebrates Research Institute Philippines ( LAMAVE ) , an NGO that conducts marine research around the country , including a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ was promoted to head of the local patrol unit and research assistant for LAMAVE 's turtle project . <p> Santo Ni ? o is located in San Vicente , an agriculture and fishing municipality that is a vital nesting ground for olive ridley sea turtles , classified as vulnerable on the IUCN Red List . <p> However , nesting turtles here face a myriad of challenges . The most prevalent is a largely unchecked stray dog population , but higher tides and more frequent storms caused by climate change loom large , as do plans to develop the area for mass tourism . <h> " <p> We still want the turtles to nest in their regular , natural place where they want to nest , because mama knows best . But it calls for an expansion of community efforts . We need people to know and own the responsibility of taking care of the turtles . <h> On patrol <p> During peak nesting season , volunteers and local patrollers walk more than 8 kilometres ( about 5 miles ) in five shifts each night and early morning . <p> When @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to finish nesting , shooing away dogs when necessary . Before she re-enters the water , they 'll measure her carapace , take photos of the unique scute pattern on her head , which acts as a fingerprint that can be used to identify her later , and take a biopsy from her fin for future genetic sampling . <p> Sometimes , they find a nesting turtle that encountered a dog , bleeding in the sand , fin torn away from her body , exposing smooth bone underneath . In those cases , they wait for the injured turtle to take her last breath , so they can remove the eggs and take them to one of their two hatcheries . <p> " Some people cope better with that than others , " researcher Klara Simon said . " In the end , even though she 's dead , at least her babies survive . " <p> When they find a nest , the eggs are dug up , counted , placed carefully in a bucket and brought to a hatchery . In past seasons , researchers found that most @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " on the beach were eaten by dogs or flooded by the tide . <p> Inside the hatchery , rows of nests make small circles in the sand , circumscribed by green , plastic netting . A researcher recreates the dimensions of the original nest and places the eggs in . They check on the nests daily every few hours . <p> About two months after the eggs are laid , they will start to hatch . In the early mornings or evenings , researchers and local patrollers take the hatchlings that surfaced that day , sometimes a hundred at once , and place them on the shore , where they begin their race to the West Philippine Sea . During this crawl , scientists believe the turtles are able to take a geomagnetic imprint , allowing them to return to the region where they were born . <p> Once in the sea , only about one in 1,000 will survive until adulthood . That survivor will spend up to 15 years at sea before reaching sexual maturity and making the journey back to the island where it was born @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ much change . <p> San Vicente has seen an increase in tourism in recent years . Century Properties , a luxury developer , purchased 56 hectares ( 138.4 acres ) , with plans to build a beachfront resort . Another major developer , Megaworld Corporation , has broken ground on " Paragua Coastown , " expected to be 462 hectares ( 1,142 acres ) . On its website , Megaworld advertises San Vicente as " an undiscovered haven ready to welcome you , " with mock-ups of solar panel clad condominiums and paved tree-lined roads built over the beach . <p> Residents , according to Mahusay , have mixed feelings about more development . <p> " Excited , but sometimes nervous , " he said . <h> The threat of dogs <p> The dogs , currently the biggest threat to the turtles , will likely remain . Many of the dogs are cared for by residents , while others are strays . The distinction is challenging , as there is no veterinarian in the area , so microchipping , spaying and neutering is not common . <p> While the Philippines @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , vaccinated and leashed , the enforcement and impounding of strays is up to local governments . San Vicente has a local ordinance that prohibits the presence of strays and authorises an animal shelter , but the ordinance is not enforced and the municipality lacks the resources to build and staff an animal shelter . <p> Researchers have sought other solutions . They noticed a concentration of dead nesters and predated nests in one portion of the beach , so they approached the land 's caretaker , Reymundo Munding , who owns eight dogs . <p> " We talked to him and we included him in the patrols , " project leader Kalei Suza said . <p> Munding , speaking through a translator , said he 's lived on the property for 15 years . Sometimes he goes out to fish for squid at night , leaving the dogs out to protect his family . <p> One morning , around 3 a.m. , patrollers found a dying turtle near his property . They waited five hours for her to die . Later , as more researchers joined the patrollers , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the turtle . <p> " He walked with us , and he was so heartbroken , because he 's seen how much effort we 're doing , that we do n't really have sleep , " Suza said . " Then boom : He asked us , ' what can we do for this to stop ? ' " <p> Munding agreed to let LAMAVE build a fenced enclosure on his property to contain the dogs at night to protect the turtles . <p> However , many residents do not want their dogs fenced in , said Santo Ni ? o Barangay Secretary Veronica Estropia . So , for now , the dogs remain , making human intervention crucial for hatchlings to survive on the quiet beaches of San Vicente . <h> The threat of storms <p> Meanwhile , storms and high tides present another rising threat to the hatchlings . <p> " It 's not natural for Palawan to be hit by typhoons , but because of climate change , a lot of storms have been hitting Palawan directly , " Suza said . <p> In January , the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it to the locations of the nests found during patrols . If the nests had n't been relocated , they would 've flooded . <p> " These animals have evolved to survive despite having a lot of calamities around them , but since we , as humans , are present , they now face a lot of threats , " Suza said . <h> The future <p> Yet , the researchers remain hopeful , especially about the ways the community has engaged with the project . Researcher Emelie Gottschling recalled a time that one of the local patrollers came to the project house , where the researchers live and work for the duration of the project , on his day off . <p> " He came with a bike to inform us that other locals found a nest on the beach , " she said . " We went there together , and they had put a blanket on top of the nest , secured with stones , to mark where the nest was and to protect it from the dogs . " <p> Together , they relocated the nest @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the Sea Turtle Conservancy , based in the US state of Florida , said relocating eggs to hatcheries should only be used as a last resort , as the move can agitate the eggs and some hatcheries are mismanaged . It 's a good sign , he said , if the people running a hatchery release the hatchlings quickly after they emerge . As for LAMAVE 's program , the photo identification and genetic data could offer more insight into the movement of olive ridley turtles in the Philippines , Valverde said . <p> According to LAMAVE 's data from last season and preliminary data from this season , in the hatcheries , around 75 per cent of the eggs in a nest will hatch . To compare , when untouched by predators , hatching success rates of 80 per cent or higher are common for in situ olive ridley nests . <p> But hatchery management , Suza said , is not the end goal . <p> " We still want the turtles to nest in their regular , natural place where they want to nest , because mama knows @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for an expansion of community efforts . We need people to know and own the responsibility of taking care of the turtles . " <p> Despite the many challenges , the researchers hope that when a hatchling returns to the white beaches of San Vicente , she will arrive at a place where she and her offspring will be looked out for and protected . <h> Like this content ? @@139160312 <p> The establishment of " fossil fuel free zones " as a tool for mitigating the climate crisis has been contested for more than a decade . <p> But in April , when representatives of 57 countries met in Colombia for the first Transition Away conference , something shifted . They were listed in the desired outcomes -- the first time a major climate conference had platformed them this significantly . <p> Fossil fuel free zones are areas of significant ecology , biodiversity or culture , where fossil fuel exploration , extraction and other related infrastructural development are permanently prohibited . <p> Dialogue Earth consulted Juan Pablo Osornio , engagement and policy director at Earth Insight , an advocacy group dedicated to protecting key environments from industrial expansion . He says fossil fuel free zones offer " something concrete , very intuitive " that governments can use in their energy transitions . <p> " We 're at a point where everyone is demanding that things be put into concrete terms , " agrees Carolina S ? nchez , a spokesperson for the Fossil Free Wider Caribbean ( GCLF ) network @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ transition roadmaps that " include dates and set out more specific details of how things are going to be done ... fossil fuel free zones are a concrete thing that already exists and countries should expand . " <h> " <p> Everything that is left in the subsoil must stay there . It fulfils an important function for our spiritual management of cooling the world . When those resources are extracted , elements of great importance for the planet 's balance are altered . <p> Patricia Su ? rez , Indigenous leader , Colombia Murui <p> Fossil fuel expansion is threatening important forests . In April , Earth Insight released an analysis of governmental data of oil and gas blocks at various stages of development and production , mapped onto satellite imagery of tropical moist forest cover in the Amazon , the Congo basin and Southeast Asia . Earth Insight says the overlap between the two amounts to 179 million hectares , and approximately 21 per cent of the " high-integrity tropical forests " in those regions . <p> The Transition Away conference took place in the coastal city of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ elsewhere in the Caribbean , Guyana has reignited offshore oil expansion , prompting Barbados , Jamaica and the Dominican Republic to take similar action . <p> A country 's route to fossil fuel free zones is littered with obstacles , such as pendulum-swinging politics and fossil-dependent economies . But increasing numbers of governments still recognise the concept as a path away from extraction . <h> How could fossil fuel free zones be adopted ? <p> Fossil fuel free zones have been proposed by campaigners and academics for over a decade . The Leave it in the Ground ( Lingo ) initiative , an anti-fossil fuels NGO , was one of their first proponents . <p> In 2022 , the academic Fergus Green -- currently a professor of public policy at University College London -- created an introductory framework for them . It was partly inspired by Nuclear Weapon Free Zones . This treaty-based arrangement was first proposed for consideration at the UN general assembly in 1999 ; Nuclear Weapon Free Zones currently cover Africa , Latin America and the Caribbean , the South Pacific and Central and Southeast Asia . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ free zones should be " enshrined in national laws and international agreements and accompanied by measures to ensure an equitable distribution of burdens and benefits " . <p> He acknowledges , however , that this will not happen before smaller-scale actions that apply pressure to states . As such , he sees the fossil fuel free zones concept as a vehicle , to " inject a set of ideas into the public consciousness on which motivated groups might draw " to create change . <p> The legal mechanisms by which fossil fuel free zones could be introduced fall into roughly four categories : self-declaration ( communities formally declare their territories off-limits ) ; administrative protected area designation ; litigation that enforces existing laws ; and political moratoria ( national or regional bans on new licences ) . <p> S ? nchez says fossil fuel free zones are often based on pre-existing laws . This has the benefit of low regulatory costs compared to some other state-led climate change mitigation projects . For example , establishing one of these zones is cheaper and easier than building out a new energy system @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be drawn on a map using existing authority . <p> However , while design might be simple , implementation is harder . Osornio says enforcement remains the challenge in many regions , particularly in Latin America and Sub-Saharan Africa , where governance capacity varies widely . <p> In Ecuador , for example , the state has continued to drill for oil in parts of the Yasun ? national park for years , despite its protected status : the country voted to completely halt extraction at the park 's ITT oil field in a 2023 referendum . Implementing that decision has proven complex and slow . <p> This is also relevant for marine protected areas ( MPAs ) . Bruna Campos , the senior offshore oil and gas campaigner at the Center for International Environmental Law ( CIEL ) , tells Dialogue Earth that many marine protected areas are merely " paper parks " . She says oil and gas activities -- whether inside or outside -- can undermine an MPA 's very purpose . <h> Where are they already being used ? <p> Lingo already has a global database of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Such sites include : Belize , which has an indefinite moratorium on offshore oil activity ; Costa Rica , with its moratorium on fossil fuel exploration and exploitation until 2050 ; Arctic Canadian waters , where there is a moratorium on oil and gas licensing , reviewed every five years ; and five particularly biodiverse areas of Mexico , each granted a fossil fuel exploration and exploitation ban . <p> In 2025 , Colombia banned new oil projects in its Amazon . And last month , it declared the Sierra Nevada de Santa Marta , the sacred territory of four Indigenous nations , liberated from fossil fuel extraction . But this legacy is already at risk from Colombia 's just-elected new president , Abelardo de la Espriella , who has called for a national resurgence of fossil fuels . <p> Fossil fuel free zones often overlap with Indigenous territories , and Indigenous people can be a force for their introduction . " Everything that is left in the subsoil must stay there . <p> It fulfils an important function for our spiritual management of cooling the world , " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Murui Indigenous community , tells Dialogue Earth . " When those resources are extracted , elements of great importance for the planet 's balance are altered , creating strong climate impacts with serious effects on Indigenous territories . " <p> Across the Amazon , Indigenous organisations have been advancing similar declarations . Last year at the UN climate summit COP30 , held in Brazil , Indigenous coalitions demanded their territories be designated exclusion zones for all extractive activities . Meanwhile , parliamentarians from four Amazonian countries have introduced bills to expand fossil fuel free zones . <p> But this political progress is fragile . " In my country , and across much of Latin America , the political shift brought about by recent elections means this idea will face greater difficulties , " Cecilia Requena , a Bolivian senator and member of Parliamentarians for a Fossil Free Future , tells Dialogue Earth . She argues protecting critical biomes like the Amazon makes just as much sense for economies as for the climate . <p> Dialogue Earth spoke to Alana Manchineri , an Indigenous leader representing Amazonian Indigenous organisations . Her @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ : " This is our mandate , and it 's the most concrete contribution we can make to cooling the planet . " <h> What challenges need to be overcome ? <p> The Santa Marta conference certainly put fossil fuel free zones on the map . Participating countries committed to developing post-conference roadmaps that will guide their fossil fuel production phaseouts , which could make use of such zones . The roadmaps will consider areas that could be prioritised for extraction bans , timelines and financing plans . <p> Fossil fuel free zones are therefore seen by proponents as an early step towards a broader phaseout , rather than an end in themselves . " If governments want to prioritise , or are thinking about how that phaseout is going to look -- because it wo n't happen all at once -- the first thing is to stop producing in these sensitive areas , " Campos adds . <p> The core problem many countries face is an economy that depends on fossil fuels , although the degree of dependence varies widely . In some countries , oil and gas revenues @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The second challenge is political inconsistency . Fossil fuel bans may not survive a change of government , as is now likely in Colombia . Durability requires anchoring these bans in stronger legal frameworks . Not just policy statements or executive orders , but statutes and international agreements that can survive political transitions . <p> Brazil has retained the UN 's climate conference presidency since hosting COP30 and as such , it is preparing a global phaseout roadmap to present at COP31 this November . It could include fossil fuel free zones but -- unlike negotiated COP decisions -- the roadmap will not be formally adopted by countries , nor carry legal or political authority under the UN . <p> Su ? rez says states must introduce strong policies and fulfil their promises . And she remains hopeful , too : " At Santa Marta , we saw the possibility opening up to the world to initiate a new context -- a new political process on the transition beyond fossils . <p> This article was originally published on Dialogue Earth under a Creative Commons licence . <h> Like this content @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to strengthen independent journalism , which is critically needed to guide business and policy development for positive impact . @@139252627 <p> Singapore 's sovereign wealth fund GIC is actively seeking opportunities in climate adaptation and resilience businesses , even as it continues to invest in the energy transition . <p> At the same time , GIC regularly screens and stress-tests its portfolio for material risks resulting from climate impact . @@139252786 <h> El Ni ? o and encroachment threaten Sri Lanka 's national parks <h> Encroachments of national parks in Sri Lanka had occurred as early as the 1950s and 1960s , shrinking park terrain . <p> Studies indicate that the resettlement of those displaced as a result of the nearly three-decade long armed conflict had contributed significantly to the deforestation in national parks such as Wilpattu . Image : Yves Alarie , CC BY-SA 3.0 , via Unsplash . <p> Around 14 per cent of Sri Lanka 's total land area is dedicated to wildlife protected areas , which includes more than 20 national parks . <p> However , due to natural and anthropogenic factors , protected species such as leopards and elephants living in these protected areas face continuous threats as their habitats continue to shrink . Wildlife authorities say they have worked hard to strengthen law enforcement in the past decade to reduce encroachment of national parks and other protected areas but have had limited success . <p> As Sri Lanka braces for an El Ni ? o that is expected to hit the Indian Ocean island hard @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ over managing animal populations inside these protected areas . <h> A decade of encroachments <p> The history of encroachment in Sri Lankan national parks dates to the colonial era and resettlement pressures following the armed conflict , which ended in May 2009 . <p> Srinath Dissanayake , a wildlife ranger with the Department of Wildlife Conservation ( DWC ) , said that over the past 10 years , there had been limited encroachments in most national parks except for parks situated near the former conflict zones in the island 's North and East . <h> " <p> Grasslands are required for elephants and many other herbivores . There must be human interventions to manage grasslands . If it 's just protected and nothing is being done , the grasslands would one day become dry zone forests . <p> Mayuri Wijesinghe , professor , University of Colombo <p> " The boundaries of most nature reserves and national parks were demarcated according to the Flora and Fauna Protection Ordinance ( FFPO ) No. 2 of 1937 , which came into operation in 1938 , " he told Mongabay . <p> For example @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in 2019 , according to new regulations . " The DWC offices were established during 1980 -- 1990 , and most of them were set up in the Northern and Eastern provinces following the end of the armed conflict . Most encroachments that we see today had happened back in the 1950s and 1960s , " he said . <p> He added that due to limited interventions by law enforcement officers , many national parks and nature reserves in the North and East were adversely impacted due to human encroachments . " Many protected areas in the North were gazetted following the end of the armed conflict , " he said . <p> The resettlement of internally displaced persons led to deforestation , paving the way for housing schemes and new roads in protected areas such as Wilpattu National Park , he said . <p> According to Dissanayake , there are ongoing negotiations about encroachment in certain areas . " Eviction orders too have been issued on numerous occasions and certain lands have been reclaimed by the DWC . But the natural surroundings will not emerge just because we reclaim @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ carry out forest regeneration in agricultural lands that have been reclaimed and such initiatives are now being implemented , " he said . <h> Managing grasslands in national parks <p> A 2021 study recorded changes in habitat coverage from 2005 to 2019 at Udawalawe National Park in southern Sri Lanka . The research assessed temporal changes in three habitat types : Forest , scrub and grassland . The results indicated that the areas under forest and scrub habitats had increased , while the grasslands had decreased . <p> Study coauthor Mayuri Wijesinghe , a senior professor with the department of zoology and environment sciences at the University of Colombo , said researchers observed how the grasslands had reduced due to natural succession and not due to any human impacts . <p> " Any habitat that is left unattended will automatically progress towards becoming a forest . The grasslands had been invaded by scrub , and now , the grasslands are low , " she told Mongabay . " Grasslands are required for elephants and many other herbivores . There must be human interventions to manage grasslands . If it 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ would one day become dry zone forests . If elephants and herbivores need to be kept inside a national park , it is essential then grasslands should remain as grasslands . " <p> According to Wijesinghe , prescribed burning is one way of managing a national park particularly for elephants and other herbivores . " This is done before the rains begin to allow a new growth of grass , " she said . " About 60-70 per cent of an elephant 's diet is mainly grass . If they do n't have enough grass , they will go about raiding crops , eventually resulting in the human-elephant conflict . " <p> Wijesinghe said when a grassland is left alone , scrubs begin to emerge . " The initial scrubs are particularly the invasive species . Invasive control also opens grasslands , " she said . <p> A 2024 audit report that evaluated grassland conservation in Sri Lanka 's national parks showed an increased threat to grasslands . The report noted how the unavailability of grass led to human-elephant conflict and crop damage by wild elephants . <p> Several other @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , the report found , including the spread of " invasive alien species , " illegal cultivation and settlements , loss of food fer herbivores due to " unauthorized entry of cattle " into national parks and maintenance of unauthorized cattle ranches in national parks . <h> Concerns over impending El Ni ? o <p> It is not just encroachment that is posing a challenge to the survival of the species , as animals in national parks are becoming vulnerable to extreme heat and evolving climate change . <p> According to environmentalist Palitha Wickramaratne , many animals in and around Yala National Park in the South have become extremely vulnerable to the prevailing heat . " However , this is not a new phenomenon because Sri Lanka has experienced drought periods from time to time since 1950s , " he told Mongabay . <p> One of the major river diversion projects in Sri Lanka was the 2005 Menik Ganga Diversion project . As part of this project , the Weheragala Reservoir was constructed to provide water for upper catchment areas of Kataragama , a town located around 15 kilometres ( @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Menik Ganga river provides water for many animals in Yala Block I , he said . <p> The small water bodies within Yala National Park , such as Heen Wewa and Gonagala Wewa were restored to ensure water storage during the drought period . " Sri Lanka faced severe droughts during 2001 , 2012 and 2016 , " he said . " In 2016 , DWC officials and various volunteer groups took water bowsers into national parks and filled the natural water bodies . Tanks and lakes that had already dried up were cleared , and mechanisms were put in place to extract groundwater . " <p> Sri Lanka 's national parks and natural attractions are major drivers of tourism revenue as people come to see cornerstone species . While there is no tracking of wildlife tourism 's independent contribution to the economy by the Central Bank of Sri Lanka , in 2025 , the island welcomed nearly 2.4 million visitors and generated an overall US$3.2 billion in total tourism revenue . <p> Sri Lankan elephants remain the primary attraction , with a single elephant generating significant income and according @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Miththapala , wild elephants are an economic asset with an individual value of about Sri Lankan rupees 4. 5 million ( US$27,000 ) . <p> Meanwhile , Wickramaratne said that if drought conditions are not addressed , it would leave a strong impact on wild animal populations within national parks . " Tourists visit national parks to witness animals in their natural surroundings . Therefore , there is a need for adequate interventions , " he told Mongabay " There are predictions of an El Ni ? o , and the dry zone , including Yala , is highly vulnerable to extreme heat conditions . " <p> One possible solution to ensure animals have adequate water is to close Yala Block I , the frequently visited section of the national park , so animals are free to move about and reach water bodies without any disturbances , Wickramaratne said . <h> Room for natural selection <p> El Ni ? o , however , is not a new phenomenon for the conservation officers , DWC 's director general Ranjan Marasinghe told Mongabay . <p> He said that droughts occur every four @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ measures to support the animal population . <p> According to him , every national park is equipped with an irrigation system of its own . " The water pumps are being repaired from time to time , and water is also provided in bowsers as well , " he said . <p> " However , we need to allow nature 's course to take place . Even though many individuals claim that animals should be provided with water when we are drought-hit , we also should consider the science behind it , " he added . " Wild animals develop a ruggedness during drought conditions and that is required to strike a balance in the ecosystem . " <p> For example , wild buffalo populations in national parks are controlled during drought periods . " This happens due to competition among prey populations for water and grasslands , " he said . <p> According to Marasinghe , DWC has plans to increase patrols during the drought period to monitor poaching and other illegal activities taking place inside the national parks . @@139290191 <h> Haryana Reviews NCR Air Pollution Measures Ahead of Winter <p> India 's Ministry of Environment , Forest and Climate Change and the Haryana government have reviewed the state 's preparedness to reduce air pollution across the National Capital Region ( NCR ) before the winter season . During a high-level meeting on 24 July 2026 , Union Environment Minister Bhupender Yadav and Haryana Chief Minister Nayab Singh Saini assessed progress on road dust management , electric mobility , industrial emissions , waste handling and crop residue management . According to the official release , the discussions focused on accelerating implementation and improving coordination among agencies . <h> Focus on coordinated action before winter <p> Senior officials from the central and Haryana governments reviewed pollution control measures for the NCR ahead of winter . Image credit : PIB . <p> The meeting included senior officials from the Ministry of Environment , Forest and Climate Change , the Commission for Air Quality Management , the Central Pollution Control Board and the Haryana State Pollution Control Board . The minister stressed that the period before winter should be used to implement pollution-control @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ delivery . <p> Authorities reviewed measures to reduce road dust , including repairing damaged roads during the monsoon period , identifying potholes , improving pavements and increasing roadside greenery with local plant species . The minister also directed officials to address gaps in the deployment of mechanical road sweeping machines by September and recommended geo-tagging the equipment to improve real-time monitoring and accountability . <h> Electric mobility and traffic management priorities <p> The review highlighted the need to accelerate the deployment of electric buses across municipal corporations in Haryana 's NCR districts . Officials noted progress in expanding electric vehicle charging infrastructure in seven municipal corporation areas . The minister also called for faster implementation of the ' Parivartan Yojana ' , which aims to replace older polluting vehicles with electric alternatives . <p> To support the ' No PUCC , No Fuel ' initiative , the minister requested the installation of automatic number plate recognition cameras at fuel stations to improve compliance monitoring . The meeting also examined urban traffic congestion and directed officials to identify and address traffic hotspots on a priority basis . <p> The emphasis on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ administration in India , including programmes linked to digital governance across Panchayati Raj institutions . The use of geo-tagging and automated compliance systems is increasingly being adopted to improve operational oversight and service delivery . <h> Industrial emissions and waste management measures <p> Industrial pollution controls were another key area of discussion . The minister directed authorities to speed up the installation of online continuous emissions monitoring systems and air pollution control devices in industrial facilities . Officials were also instructed to accelerate the establishment of secondary collection centres for construction and demolition waste . <p> The meeting additionally reviewed legacy waste removal and municipal solid waste management .
